Inc., 1992 1 \n    39→\n    40→--- PAGE 4 ---\n    41→IN I - TECHNOLOGY ~ \n    42→HOW DO YOU ACHIEVE HIGH QUALITY SOLDER JOINTS? \n    43→The process of producing high quality solder joints is \n    44→similar to the process of learning how to ride a bicycle. \n    45→If you do not understand the fundamentals, it is virtually \n    46→impossible. However, once you understand and imple- \n    47→ment the basics, the results will follow. The goal of all \n    48→SMT process engineers is to repeatedly produce high \n    49→quality solder joints that have fine grain structure and \n    50→excellent wetting characteristics, as shown in the figure \n    51→below: \n    52→SURFACES MUST BE \n    53→CLEAN AND SOLDERABLE \n    54→II MUST BE \n    55→11 SMALL \n    56→CONTROL VARIABLES \n    57→In order to produce high quality solderjoints, all process \n    58→variables must be in control. The five general process \n    59→variables (l) that need to be in control include the mate- \n    60→rials, machines, manpower, methods, and the environ- \n    61→ment. If the process variables are not in control, there is \n    62→a very good chance that soldering defects will exist. \n    63→Let’s take a closer look at each. \n    64→MATERIALS \n    65→The materials must meet functional specifications so \n    66→that the product will perform to specification. Of equal \n    67→importance is the solderability of the assembly. The \n    68→solder paste/flux system must be compatible with the \n    69→base materials of the parts to be soldered. The solder \n    70→paste and parts should be as fresh as possible to enhance \n    71→solderability. \n    72→MACHINES \n    73→The machines used in the process must be able to perform \n    74→to their specifications. The machines should operate \n    75→within the range for which they are designed. Pushing a \n    76→machine beyond its operating limits will ultimately cause \n    77→a problem. The machines must be reliable and service- \n    78→able. \n    79→MANPOWER \n    80→The manpower must be properly trained. All machines in \n    81→the line require knowledgeable operators and mainte- \n    82→nance personnel. A machine is only as good as the person \n    83→operating it. \n    84→METHODS \n    85→The methods and procedures must be defined and strictly \n    86→adhered to. Inventory procedures should be implemented \n    87→to ensure that components and boards do not stay on the \n    88→shelf too long. Problems should be fully documented \n    89→including all data that pertains to it. \n    90→ENVIRONMENT \n    91→Finally, the processing environment must be controlled. \n    92→This includes both the environment inside and outside of \n    93→the machines. For example, if the humidity in the solder- \n    94→ing area is too high, this can cause the solder paste to \n    95→absorb moisture, whichmay cause problems duringreflow . \n    96→If the temperature in the room is not controlled, the \n    97→thermal profile in the reflow oven may be affected, which \n    98→again may cause a defect. \n    99→The purpose of this text is to address one aspect of high \n   100→quality SMT soldering, the reflow process. \n   101→Producing high quality solder joints \n   102→requires that five general process \n   103→variables need to be in control; the \n   104→materials, machines, manpower, \n   105→methods, and the environment. \n   106→2 Section I - Technology \n   107→\n   108→--- PAGE 5 ---\n   109→WHAT IS THE IMPORTANCE OF THE PROFILE? \n   110→I I I I I I 1; I :I I I I I \n   111→In order to fully understand Surface Mount Technology \n   112→(SMT) reflow, one needs to have an understanding of \n   113→what is being accomplished. One can take too simplistic \n   114→a viewpoint in that all you need to do is to heat solder \n   115→to a temperature greater than its melting point. One can \n   116→also take too complicated a viewpoint in believing that a \n   117→certain wavelength of infrared (IR) is required, or that a \n   118→certain percentage of convection is required. Perhaps the \n   119→most astute comment made on surface mount reflow was \n   120→by an individual whom had just completed an evaluation \n   121→of vapor phase, panel IR, and lamp IR equipment nearly \n   122→five years ago. After coming up with virtually identical \n   123→soldering defect results on all three types of soldering \n   124→equipment, the following comment was made: “If you \n   125→could hit the correct reflow profile using buckets of hot \n   126→water, you would accomplish high quality, low defect \n   127→solder joints.” Although the above comment was made \n   128→in jest (hot water is not quite hot enough to reflow solder), \n   129→there is considerable truth to what was said. \n   130→I \n   131→REFLOW PROFILE REQUIREMENT \n   132→High quality, low defect soldering requires identifying \n   133→the optimum temperature profile for reflowing the solder \n   134→paste, and repeating over and over. Every solder joint on \n   135→every board, needs to be heated similarily if the desired \n   136→soldering results are to be accomplished. It does not \n   137→matter how you apply the heat to the solder joint. What \n   138→does matter is that the heat is applied to the solder joint \n   139→in a controlled manner. The heating and cooling rise \n   140→rates must be compatible with the solder paste and \n   141→components. The amount of time that the assembly is \n   142→exposed to certain temperatures must be defined and \n   143→maintained. In other words, the solder reflow profile \n   144→must first be defined and then maintained. \n   145→280- \n   146→260- \n   147→240- \n   148→T 220- \n   149→High quality, low defect soldering \n   150→requires identifying the optimum \n   151→temperature profile for reflowing \n   152→the solder paste, and repeating it \n   153→over and over. \n   154→I WETTING ; ‘ TIME , \n   155→30-60 .’ : SEC ~ \n   156→TYPICAL PROFILE \n   157→A typical profile consists of a preheat, dryout, and reflow \n   158→section (zone) where different heating rates are used. In \n   159→the preheat section, the goal is to fully preheat the SMT \n   160→assembly to temperatures generally between 100°C to \n   161→150°C. The most critical parameter in the preheat section \n   162→is to minimize the rate of rise to less than 2”C/second. \n   163→The main concern is minimizing thermal shock on the \n   164→components of the assembly. Multilayer ceramic chip \n   165→capacitors can be vulnerable to cracking if heated too \n   166→fast. Solder paste can spatter also, if heated too fast. \n   167→The second heating section, referred to as the dryout, \n   168→soak, or preflow zone, is used primarily to ensure that \n   169→the solder paste is fully dried before hitting reflow \n   170→temperatures. This zone also acts as a flux activation \n   171→zone for some solder pastes. Solder reflow is accom- \n   172→plished in the reflow zone, where the solder paste is \n   173→elevated to a temperature greater than the melting point \n   174→of the solder. For 63/37 Eutectic solder, the melting \n   175→temperature is 183°C. This temperature must be ex- \n   176→ceeded by approximately 20°C to ensure quality reflow. \n   177→The amount of time the solder joint is above the melting \n   178→point is referred to as the “wetting time”. The wetting \n   179→time is normally 30 to 60 seconds for most pastes. If the \n   180→wetting time is excessive, intermetallics may occur in \n   181→the joint, which result in brittle solder joints. In sum- \n   182→mary, define the profile, and hit it. \n   183→Section I - Technology 3 \n   184→\n   185→--- PAGE 6 ---\n   186→HOW MANY HEATING ZONES ARE REQUIRED? \n   187→This question frequently arrives and can be a source of \n   188→confusion. One theory is that if ten zones are good, then \n   189→twenty zones are better. While in theory this may be true, \n   190→in practicality it may not be true. While it improves the \n   191→flexibility of oven, it also adds to the complexity and \n   192→makes oven set-up more difficult. \n   193→Let’s review this question from the standpoint of how \n   194→many zones are really needed. Reflowing solder paste \n   195→typically requires two or three different heating rates to \n   196→accomplish the process. In the figure below, there are \n   197→three “typical” profile bands for RMA, water soluble, \n   198→and no-clean solder pastes. These profiles are rather \n   199→generic, and one should consult with their solder paste \n   200→vendor to determine the desired profile. All of the pastes \n   201→in the figure could be successfully reflowed using \n   202→three zones. \n   203→reflow is that if you can control your top and bottom \n   204→heaters separately, then double sided reflow can be \n   205→accomplished (without reflowing side A twice). This \n   206→will not work with most SMT reflow processes, as most \n   207→products are too thermally thin. Independent topbottom \n   208→heating control does help in machines with dual convey- \n   209→ors that are located at different heights in the chamber. \n   210→Product warpage may also be minimized through proper \n   211→control. The bottom line is that most machines have this \n   212→feature, but most often the top and bottom heaters will be \n   213→set at the same value. \n   214→Edge heating zones become significant when edge con- \n   215→veyors are used. Edge conveyors act as heat sinks on the \n   216→edge of the product. Two zones of edge heat (one front, \n   217→one back) will assist in compensating for edge con- \n   218→veyors. If mesh conveyors are used, these zones are \n   219→unnecessary unless the product width is quite wide. \n   220→A second issue is the question of independent top and \n   221→bottom heat control. A common conception in SMT \n   222→200 c \n   223→I PREHEAT1 I PREHEAT2 I REFLOW \n   224→I \n   225→I \n   226→I \n   227→I \n   228→I \n   229→I \n   230→I \n   231→I \n   232→I \n   233→I \n   234→I \n   235→COOLING I \n   236→TIME \n   237→4 Section I - Technology \n   238→\n   239→--- PAGE 7 ---\n   240→WHAT IS THE PROFILE PROCESS BAND? \n   241→In reality the profile is not a line, but rather a band or \n   242→process window.(*) The size of the band is defined by the \n   243→range of temperature deviation that can occur during \n   244→reflow and yield high quality results. This is indicated \n   245→by the crosshatched area shown in the figure. In the \n   246→example, the profile band size is indicated as 25°C. \n   247→The actual size of the band will vary depending upon \n   248→solder paste, component type, and circuit board material. \n   249→SOLDER SPATTER \n   250→AND SOLDER BALLS \n   251→CAUSED BY INSUF- \n   252→FICIENT DRYING. \n   253→BURNT BOARDS, \n   254→DEWET SOLDER \n   255→fi COLD SOLDER \n   256→JOINTS, DULL \n   257→SOLDER JOINTS \n   258→(TOO COLD) \n   259→REFLOW SOLDERING DEFECTS \n   260→If the product temperature profile is not maintained \n   261→within the band, a defect will occur. Chip capacitors \n   262→may crack if the rise rate in the preheat zone is exces- \n   263→sive. Solder spatter and solder balls may occur if drying \n   264→is excessive or insufficient. If drying is excessive, the \n   265→solder paste may skin over and not allow any of the \n   266→volatiles to escape. As the temperature of the solder joint \n   267→continues to heat, the vapor pressure increases until the \n   268→solder joint virtually explodes as the gases escape rap- \n   269→idly. The result is solder spatter and solder balls. If the \n   270→preheat is insufficient, solder spatter and balls may occur \n   271→in the reflow zone when higher heating rates occur. It is \n   272→critical that the paste is dried at low controlled heating \n   273→rates. In the reflow zone, insufficient heat will result in \n   274→non-reflowed or partially reflowed solder joints. The \n   275→joints will have a dull, rough surface finish in this case. \n   276→Excessive heating in the reflow zone may result in burnt \n   277→boards, damaged components, or possibly solder \n   278→dewetting. In a dewet solder joint, the solder is reflowed \n   279→but it is pulled away from the pad or the component lead. \n   280→DEFINE THE BAND \n   281→The profile band needs to be defined for the given SMT \n   282→assembly. To define this band, one should be aware of \n   283→not only the solder paste requirements, but also any \n   284→specific requirements of components or the circuit board \n   285→material. The process band width is defined as the total \n   286→deviation in temperature that can occur and yield reliable \n   287→results. \n   288→OVEN PERFORMANCE \n   289→From a process standpoint, the reflow oven must be able \n   290→to produce consistent results within the reflow profile \n   291→band. The user should be aware of all variables that \n   292→influence the band. These variables may be defined as \n   293→product related and oven related. By far the most critical \n   294→product related non-uniformity is that created by large \n   295→mass differences on the product. Since it is easier to heat \n   296→an area that has no components as compared with an area \n   297→with large components, temperature differentials will \n   298→exist on the product. Machine related non-uniformities \n   299→such as repeatability under product load and edge-to- \n   300→center and front-to-back product heating uniformities \n   301→must also be considered. To achieve high quality, low \n   302→defect soldering results, the sum of all the non-uniformi- \n   303→ties must fall within the defined reflow profile band. \n   304→In reality the profile is not a line, but rather \n   305→a range of temperatures all solder joints \n   306→must be exposed. \n   307→The total temperature deviation from \n   308→component thermal mismatch, oven load- \n   309→ing, and oven uniformity must be within \n   310→the band. \n   311→Section I - Technology 5 \n   312→\n   313→--- PAGE 8 ---\n   314→WHAT ARE THE CAUSES OF NON-UNIFORM HEATING? \n   315→In order to fully comprehend the capability to heat within \n   316→the defined process band, one needs to better understand \n   317→the causes of heating non-uniformities. The three main \n   318→causes of non-uniform heating in the SMT reflow pro- \n   319→cess include mass differential on the product, conveyor \n   320→and heater edge effects, and product loading. \n   321→MASS DIFFERENTIAL \n   322→The amount of temperature rise of a product as it travels \n   323→through a heating zone depends upon a number of \n   324→variables. The temperature rise of an object subjected to \n   325→heat is determined by the following equation: \n   326→AT = Q x AX t / (M x Cp) \n   327→WHERE: \n   328→AT = Product Temperature Rise (“C) \n   329→Q = Heat Absorbed (w/cmz) \n   330→A = Exposed Area (cm2) \n   331→t = Heating Dwell Time (seconds) \n   332→M = Mass of Object (kg) \n   333→Cp = Specific Heat (w-sec/kg-”C) \n   334→The mass (M) times the specific heat (Cp) is often referred \n   335→to as thermal mass. Greater thermal mass requires more \n   336→heat to achieve a given temperature rise. In the figure \n   337→shown below, the area populated with the PLCC’s has \n   338→considerably more thermal mass per unit area than the \n   339→area with the discrete components. This area will be more \n   340→difficult to heat. The non-uniformity caused by thermal \n   341→mass differential may be measured by thermocoupling a \n   342→lead on the largest and smallest device, and running a \n   343→profile. \n   344→HIGH MASS DIFFERENTIAL \n   345→J \n   346→mmmm \n   347→mmmm \n   348→mmmm \n   349→mmmm \n   350→mmmm \n   351→mmmm \n   352→OVEN UNIFORMITY \n   353→A second main cause of non-uniform product heating is \n   354→the oven heating pattern. This can be caused by edge \n   355→effects on the heaters (less heat at the end) or by heat \n   356→sinking of the conveyor system. The edge effect may be \n   357→caused by insufficient airflow around the edges of the \n   358→machine, or by non-uniformities that are inherent in the \n   359→heaters. The heat sinking of the conveyor is really amass \n   360→effect, similar to the one described above. The oven \n   361→uniformity may be measured by thermocoupling a bare \n   362→board over a matrix of points, and running a profile. The \n   363→matrix should include points on the board edges (front, \n   364→back, left, and right), and the center of the board. \n   365→OVEN REPEATABILITY \n   366→Repeatability refers to the oven’s capability to repeat a \n   367→given profile. Repeatability is affected by machine \n   368→loading. The figure below shows a typical product \n   369→loading condition. The “Loading Factor” is defined in \n   370→the figure. \n   371→PRODUCT FLOW INTO OVEN \n   372→b \n   373→L \n   374→LOADING FACTOR = L+s \n   375→The higher the value of the loading factor, the more \n   376→difficult it is for any oven to give repeatable results. \n   377→Ovens will generally specify a maximum loading factor \n   378→(ranging from 0.5 to 0.9). The loading factor becomes an \n   379→important consideration when sizing an oven. Periodically, run the test board within the oven \n   383→load and compare with the unloaded profile. \n   384→The three main causes of non-uniform heating in \n   385→the SMT reflow process include mass differential \n   386→on the product, conveyor and heater edge effects, \n   387→and product loading. \n   388→6 Section I - Technology \n   389→\n   390→--- PAGE 9 ---\n   391→WHAT ARE THE MODES OF HEATING? \n   392→There are three different heating modes involved wit \n   393→most SMT reflow processes; convection, conductior \n   394→and infrared radiation (IR). {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-sonnet-4-5-20250929","id":"msg_vrtx_01Jmp3ViRUEc2jQ4yoRN2xuA","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_vrtx_01SCtvzKZ7UUhL89yx16UgJm","name":"Bash","input":{"command":"python3 << 'EOF'\n# Create a comprehensive organized summary\n\nsummary = \"\"\"\n================================================================================\nREFLOW MACHINE MAINTENANCE HANDBOOK - EXTRACTED INFORMATION\n================================================================================\nSource: /app/data/handbook.pdf\nExtracted: 24 pages, 55,624 characters\n\n================================================================================\n1. PREHEAT PHASE DEFINITION AND TEMPERATURE RANGE\n================================================================================\n\nDEFINITION:\nThe preheat section is the first heating zone in a typical reflow profile.\nIt is used to fully preheat the SMT (Surface Mount Technology) assembly \nbefore entering the dryout and reflow zones.\n\nAPPLICABLE TEMPERATURE RANGE:\n- Target Range: 100°C to 150°C\n- Purpose: Bring the entire assembly to a uniform temperature to prepare \n  for the dryout and reflow phases\n\nKEY REQUIREMENTS:\n- The goal is to fully preheat the SMT assembly to temperatures generally \n  between 100°C to 150°C\n- Primary concern: Minimizing thermal shock on components\n- Critical for preventing damage to multilayer ceramic chip capacitors\n- Prevents solder paste spattering\n\n================================================================================\n2. MAXIMUM ALLOWABLE RAMP RATE DURING PREHEAT\n================================================================================\n\nMAXIMUM RAMP RATE: Less than 2°C/second\n\nSPECIFICATION:\n\"The most critical parameter in the preheat section is to minimize the \nrate of rise to less than 2°C/second.\"\n\nREASONING:\n- Main concern is minimizing thermal shock on components\n- Multilayer ceramic chip capacitors can be vulnerable to cracking if \n  heated too fast\n- Solder paste can spatter if heated too fast\n\nADDITIONAL NOTES:\n- At typical operating setpoints of 5-15% lamp power, heating rates are \n  maintained under 2°C/second\n- This controlled rate is critical for component reliability\n\n================================================================================\n3. EQUATION/METHOD FOR CALCULATING RAMP RATE\n================================================================================\n\nThe handbook provides the equation for temperature rise, which can be used\nto calculate and control ramp rates:\n\nTEMPERATURE RISE EQUATION:\nΔT = Q × A × t / (M × Cp)\n\nWHERE:\n- ΔT = Product Temperature Rise (°C)\n- Q = Heat Absorbed (W/cm²)\n- A = Exposed Area (cm²)\n- t = Heating Dwell Time (seconds)\n- M = Mass of Object (kg)\n- Cp = Specific Heat (W-sec/kg-°C)\n\nTHERMAL MASS CONCEPT:\n- M × Cp is referred to as \"thermal mass\"\n- Greater thermal mass requires more heat to achieve a given temperature rise\n- This equation helps predict heating behavior based on component mass \n  and heating parameters\n\nPROCESS SPEED EQUATION:\nPROCESS SPEED = OVEN CHAMBER HEATED LENGTH / PROCESS DWELL TIME\n\nLINE SPEED (MINIMUM) = BOARDS PER MINUTE × LENGTH PER BOARD / LOAD FACTOR\n\nThese equations help calculate conveyor speed to achieve desired dwell times\nand ramp rates.\n\n================================================================================\n4. TAL (TIME ABOVE LIQUIDUS) DEFINITION, THRESHOLDS, AND ALLOWABLE WINDOW\n================================================================================\n\nDEFINITION:\nThe handbook refers to this as \"wetting time\" - the amount of time the \nsolder joint is above the melting point (liquidus temperature).\n\nFor 63/37 Eutectic Solder:\n- Liquidus/Melting Temperature: 183°C\n- This temperature must be exceeded by approximately 20°C to ensure \n  quality reflow\n- Effective minimum temperature for quality reflow: ~203°C\n\nALLOWABLE WINDOW (WETTING TIME):\n- Normal Range: 30 to 60 seconds for most pastes\n- This is the time spent above the liquidus temperature (183°C)\n\nTHRESHOLDS AND WARNINGS:\n- Minimum: Less than 30 seconds may result in insufficient wetting and \n  poor solder joint quality\n- Maximum: Excessive wetting time (>60 seconds) may cause intermetallics \n  to occur in the joint, resulting in brittle solder joints\n- Optimal: 30-60 second window provides best balance\n\nQUALITY INDICATORS:\n- Proper TAL results in fine grain structure and excellent wetting \n  characteristics\n- Insufficient time: Dull, rough surface finish; non-reflowed or partially \n  reflowed joints (cold solder joints)\n- Excessive time: Intermetallic formation, brittle joints\n\n================================================================================\n5. PEAK TEMPERATURE REQUIREMENTS\n================================================================================\n\nFOR 63/37 EUTECTIC SOLDER:\n- Base Melting Point: 183°C\n- Required Exceedance: Approximately 20°C above melting point\n- Minimum Peak Temperature: ~203°C (183°C + 20°C)\n\nPURPOSE:\n- Ensures quality reflow of solder joints\n- Provides adequate temperature margin for complete wetting\n- Ensures all solder joints reach proper reflow temperature\n\nTEMPERATURE PROFILE BAND:\n- Example band size mentioned: 25°C\n- The profile is not a single line but a \"band\" or \"process window\"\n- Band size varies depending on solder paste, component type, and circuit \n  board material\n\nUPPER LIMITS (DEFECTS FROM EXCESSIVE HEATING):\n- Burnt boards\n- Damaged components\n- Solder dewetting (solder pulls away from pad or component lead)\n\nCRITICAL ZONES:\n- Product temperatures greater than 150°C require high-purity nitrogen \n  atmosphere (if using nitrogen process)\n- Pure nitrogen delivered to reflow and dryout zones for critical stages\n\n================================================================================\n6. BOARD GEOMETRY, THERMAL DWELL REQUIREMENTS, AND CONVEYOR SPEED CALCULATIONS\n================================================================================\n\nTHERMAL DWELL TIME:\nDefinition: The time a product spends in a heating zone (measured in seconds)\n\nFrom the temperature rise equation:\nt = Heating Dwell Time (seconds)\n\nThis is a critical parameter in the equation:\nΔT = Q × A × t / (M × Cp)\n\nCONVEYOR SPEED CALCULATIONS:\n\n1. MINIMUM LINE SPEED:\n   LINE SPEED (MINIMUM) = BOARDS PER MINUTE × LENGTH PER BOARD / LOAD FACTOR\n   \n   Purpose: Operating at a conveyor speed greater than the calculated \n   speed ensures that no product jams will occur on the onload area\n\n2. PROCESS SPEED:\n   PROCESS SPEED = OVEN CHAMBER HEATED LENGTH / PROCESS DWELL TIME\n   \n   The process speed must be greater than the minimum calculated line \n   speed to ensure bottlenecks will not occur at the reflow oven\n\nLOADING FACTOR:\n   LOADING FACTOR = L / (L + S)\n   \n   Where:\n   - L = Product length or spacing between products\n   - S = Space between products\n   - Higher values (0.5 to 0.9 typical) mean more difficult repeatability\n   - Maximum loading factors typically range from 0.5 to 0.9\n\nBOARD GEOMETRY CONSIDERATIONS:\n\nMass Differential Effects:\n- High mass components (e.g., PLCCs) have considerably more thermal mass \n  per unit area than discrete components\n- Areas with high thermal mass are more difficult to heat\n- Non-uniformity measurement: Thermocouple a lead on the largest and \n  smallest device and run a profile\n\nExposed Area (A):\n- Larger exposed area (cm²) in the equation allows for more heat absorption\n- Component placement affects heat distribution\n\nEdge Effects:\n- Edge conveyors act as heat sinks on the edge of the product\n- Edge heating zones may be needed to compensate\n- Mesh conveyors reduce heat sink effects unless product width is very wide\n- Only 0.17 inches (4 mm) of product edge contacts conveyor rails\n\nConveyor Design Considerations:\n- Edge conveyor straightness is critical (warpage must be <4mm to prevent \n  product falling)\n- Rails grow and warp during warm-up\n- Edge conveyors used for in-line processing or double-sided assembly\n\nUNIFORMITY REQUIREMENTS:\n- Bare board profile testing across matrix of points\n- Test points should include board edges (front, back, left, right) and center\n- Repeatability testing under loaded conditions\n\n================================================================================\n7. GUIDANCE ON SELECTING BEST RUNS BASED ON QUALITY AND EFFICIENCY\n================================================================================\n\nHIGH QUALITY SOLDER JOINT CRITERIA:\n1. Fine grain structure\n2. Excellent wetting characteristics\n3. Clean and solderable surfaces\n4. Small grain size\n\nFIVE PROCESS VARIABLES THAT MUST BE CONTROLLED:\n1. Materials - Fresh, compatible solder paste/flux system\n2. Machines - Operating within specifications, reliable, serviceable\n3. Manpower - Properly trained operators and maintenance personnel\n4. Methods - Defined procedures strictly adhered to\n5. Environment - Controlled humidity and temperature\n\nREFLOW PROFILE REQUIREMENT:\n\"High quality, low defect soldering requires identifying the optimum \ntemperature profile for reflowing the solder paste, and repeating over \nand over.\"\n\nKey principle: \"If you could hit the correct reflow profile using buckets \nof hot water, you would accomplish high quality, low defect solder joints.\"\n(The method of heating matters less than maintaining the correct profile)\n\nPROCESS BAND COMPLIANCE:\n- All solder joints on every board need to be heated similarly\n- Total temperature deviation from:\n  * Component thermal mismatch\n  * Oven loading\n  * Oven uniformity\n  Must fall within the defined reflow profile band\n\nDEFECT PREVENTION - STAY WITHIN THE BAND:\nIf temperature profile is NOT maintained within the band:\n\nPreheat Zone Issues:\n- Excessive ramp rate → Cracked chip capacitors\n- Insufficient preheat → Solder spatter and balls in reflow zone\n\nDryout Zone Issues:\n- Excessive drying → Paste skins over, vapor explosion causes spatter/balls\n- Insufficient drying → Solder spatter and balls\n\nReflow Zone Issues:\n- Insufficient heat → Non-reflowed joints, dull rough surface (cold joints)\n- Excessive heat → Burnt boards, damaged components, solder dewetting\n\nBEST RUN SELECTION CRITERIA:\n\nQuality Metrics:\n1. Profile stays within defined temperature band (e.g., ±25°C)\n2. Wetting time: 30-60 seconds\n3. Peak temperature: 183°C + 20°C = ~203°C minimum\n4. Ramp rate: <2°C/second in preheat\n5. All zones (preheat 100-150°C, dryout, reflow >203°C) properly executed\n\nEfficiency Metrics:\n1. Process speed must exceed minimum line speed\n2. Loading factor optimized (0.5-0.9 range)\n3. No bottlenecks at reflow oven\n4. Proper oven sizing for production rate\n5. Repeatability under production load\n\nEquipment Selection Criteria for Efficiency:\n- Proper oven size for production rate\n- Adequate process speed capability\n- Good repeatability (°C specification)\n- Maximum loading factor capacity\n- Appropriate conveyor type (mesh vs. edge)\n- Reliability and serviceability\n- Cost evaluation (initial + operating costs)\n\nOptimization Strategy:\n- \"Try to avoid becoming too focussed on one issue, such as populated \n  uniformity, as it may not lead you to the optimum decision.\"\n- Consider all factors holistically\n- Test and validate profile on actual production boards\n- Monitor: edge-to-center uniformity, front-to-back uniformity, \n  loaded vs unloaded conditions\n\nTYPICAL PROFILE STRUCTURE:\n1. Preheat Zone: 100-150°C, <2°C/s ramp rate\n2. Dryout/Soak Zone: Dry paste, activate flux\n3. Reflow Zone: >203°C (183°C + 20°C), 30-60s wetting time\n4. Cooling: Controlled cooling rate\n\nPROCESS GAS CONSIDERATIONS (for quality):\n- Nitrogen atmosphere may be required for:\n  * Low-residue solder paste reflow\n  * No-clean processing\n  * Enhanced solderability\n- Purity levels: 35 PPM over incoming purity in dryout/reflow zones\n- Product temperature >150°C zones require pure nitrogen\n- Recirculated gas can be used in preheat zone (purity less critical)\n\n================================================================================\nSUMMARY OF KEY NUMERICAL VALUES\n================================================================================\n\nTemperature Specifications:\n- Preheat Range: 100°C to 150°C\n- Liquidus (63/37 Eutectic): 183°C\n- Minimum Peak: ~203°C (183°C + 20°C)\n- Critical nitrogen zone: >150°C\n\nTime Specifications:\n- Wetting Time (TAL): 30 to 60 seconds\n- Typical ramp rate: <2°C/second\n\nProcess Band:\n- Example band size: ±25°C (varies by paste/components/board)\n\nPhysical Specifications:\n- Edge conveyor contact: 0.17 inches (4 mm)\n- Loading factor range: 0.5 to 0.9 (typical maximum)\n\nGas Purity (if using {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_vrtx_01SCtvzKZ7UUhL89yx16UgJm","type":"tool_result","content":"================================================================================\nREFLOW MACHINE MAINTENANCE HANDBOOK - EXTRACTED INFORMATION\n================================================================================\nSource: /app/data/handbook.pdf\nExtracted: 24 pages, 55,624 characters\n\n================================================================================\n1. PREHEAT PHASE DEFINITION AND TEMPERATURE RANGE\n================================================================================\n\nDEFINITION:\nThe preheat section is the first heating zone in a typical reflow profile.\nIt is used to fully preheat the SMT (Surface Mount Technology) assembly \nbefore entering the dryout and reflow zones.\n\nAPPLICABLE TEMPERATURE RANGE:\n- Target Range: 100°C to 150°C\n- Purpose: Bring the entire assembly to a uniform temperature to prepare \n  for the dryout and reflow phases\n\nKEY REQUIREMENTS:\n- The goal is to fully preheat the SMT assembly to temperatures generally \n  between 100°C to 150°C\n- Primary concern: Minimizing thermal shock on components\n- Critical for preventing damage to multilayer ceramic chip capacitors\n- Prevents solder paste spattering\n\n================================================================================\n2. MAXIMUM ALLOWABLE RAMP RATE DURING PREHEAT\n================================================================================\n\nMAXIMUM RAMP RATE: Less than 2°C/second\n\nSPECIFICATION:\n\"The most critical parameter in the preheat section is to minimize the \nrate of rise to less than 2°C/second.\"\n\nREASONING:\n- Main concern is minimizing thermal shock on components\n- Multilayer ceramic chip capacitors can be vulnerable to cracking if \n  heated too fast\n- Solder paste can spatter if heated too fast\n\nADDITIONAL NOTES:\n- At typical operating setpoints of 5-15% lamp power, heating rates are \n  maintained under 2°C/second\n- This controlled rate is critical for component reliability\n\n================================================================================\n3. EQUATION/METHOD FOR CALCULATING RAMP RATE\n================================================================================\n\nThe handbook provides the equation for temperature rise, which can be used\nto calculate and control ramp rates:\n\nTEMPERATURE RISE EQUATION:\nΔT = Q × A × t / (M × Cp)\n\nWHERE:\n- ΔT = Product Temperature Rise (°C)\n- Q = Heat Absorbed (W/cm²)\n- A = Exposed Area (cm²)\n- t = Heating Dwell Time (seconds)\n- M = Mass of Object (kg)\n- Cp = Specific Heat (W-sec/kg-°C)\n\nTHERMAL MASS CONCEPT:\n- M × Cp is referred to as \"thermal mass\"\n- Greater thermal mass requires more heat to achieve a given temperature rise\n- This equation helps predict heating behavior based on component mass \n  and heating parameters\n\nPROCESS SPEED EQUATION:\nPROCESS SPEED = OVEN CHAMBER HEATED LENGTH / PROCESS DWELL TIME\n\nLINE SPEED (MINIMUM) = BOARDS PER MINUTE × LENGTH PER BOARD / LOAD FACTOR\n\nThese equations help calculate conveyor speed to achieve desired dwell times\nand ramp rates.\n\n================================================================================\n4. TAL (TIME ABOVE LIQUIDUS) DEFINITION, THRESHOLDS, AND ALLOWABLE WINDOW\n================================================================================\n\nDEFINITION:\nThe handbook refers to this as \"wetting time\" - the amount of time the \nsolder joint is above the melting point (liquidus temperature).\n\nFor 63/37 Eutectic Solder:\n- Liquidus/Melting Temperature: 183°C\n- This temperature must be exceeded by approximately 20°C to ensure \n  quality reflow\n- Effective minimum temperature for quality reflow: ~203°C\n\nALLOWABLE WINDOW (WETTING TIME):\n- Normal Range: 30 to 60 seconds for most pastes\n- This is the time spent above the liquidus temperature (183°C)\n\nTHRESHOLDS AND WARNINGS:\n- Minimum: Less than 30 seconds may result in insufficient wetting and \n  poor solder joint quality\n- Maximum: Excessive wetting time (>60 seconds) may cause intermetallics \n  to occur in the joint, resulting in brittle solder joints\n- Optimal: 30-60 second window provides best balance\n\nQUALITY INDICATORS:\n- Proper TAL results in fine grain structure and excellent wetting \n  characteristics\n- Insufficient time: Dull, rough surface finish; non-reflowed or partially \n  reflowed joints (cold solder joints)\n- Excessive time: Intermetallic formation, brittle joints\n\n================================================================================\n5. PEAK TEMPERATURE REQUIREMENTS\n================================================================================\n\nFOR 63/37 EUTECTIC SOLDER:\n- Base Melting Point: 183°C\n- Required Exceedance: Approximately 20°C above melting point\n- Minimum Peak Temperature: ~203°C (183°C + 20°C)\n\nPURPOSE:\n- Ensures quality reflow of solder joints\n- Provides adequate temperature margin for complete wetting\n- Ensures all solder joints reach proper reflow temperature\n\nTEMPERATURE PROFILE BAND:\n- Example band size mentioned: 25°C\n- The profile is not a single line but a \"band\" or \"process window\"\n- Band size varies depending on solder paste, component type, and circuit \n  board material\n\nUPPER LIMITS (DEFECTS FROM EXCESSIVE HEATING):\n- Burnt boards\n- Damaged components\n- Solder dewetting (solder pulls away from pad or component lead)\n\nCRITICAL ZONES:\n- Product temperatures greater than 150°C require high-purity nitrogen \n  atmosphere (if using nitrogen process)\n- Pure nitrogen delivered to reflow and dryout zones for critical stages\n\n================================================================================\n6. BOARD GEOMETRY, THERMAL DWELL REQUIREMENTS, AND CONVEYOR SPEED CALCULATIONS\n================================================================================\n\nTHERMAL DWELL TIME:\nDefinition: The time a product spends in a heating zone (measured in seconds)\n\nFrom the temperature rise equation:\nt = Heating Dwell Time (seconds)\n\nThis is a critical parameter in the equation:\nΔT = Q × A × t / (M × Cp)\n\nCONVEYOR SPEED CALCULATIONS:\n\n1. MINIMUM LINE SPEED:\n   LINE SPEED (MINIMUM) = BOARDS PER MINUTE × LENGTH PER BOARD / LOAD FACTOR\n   \n   Purpose: Operating at a conveyor speed greater than the calculated \n   speed ensures that no product jams will occur on the onload area\n\n2. PROCESS SPEED:\n   PROCESS SPEED = OVEN CHAMBER HEATED LENGTH / PROCESS DWELL TIME\n   \n   The process speed must be greater than the minimum calculated line \n   speed to ensure bottlenecks will not occur at the reflow oven\n\nLOADING FACTOR:\n   LOADING FACTOR = L / (L + S)\n   \n   Where:\n   - L = Product length or spacing between products\n   - S = Space between products\n   - Higher values (0.5 to 0.9 typical) mean more difficult repeatability\n   - Maximum loading factors typically range from 0.5 to 0.9\n\nBOARD GEOMETRY CONSIDERATIONS:\n\nMass Differential Effects:\n- High mass components (e.g., PLCCs) have considerably more thermal mass \n  per unit area than discrete components\n- Areas with high thermal mass are more difficult to heat\n- Non-uniformity measurement: Thermocouple a lead on the largest and \n  smallest device and run a profile\n\nExposed Area (A):\n- Larger exposed area (cm²) in the equation allows for more heat absorption\n- Component placement affects heat distribution\n\nEdge Effects:\n- Edge conveyors act as heat sinks on the edge of the product\n- Edge heating zones may be needed to compensate\n- Mesh conveyors reduce heat sink effects unless product width is very wide\n- Only 0.17 inches (4 mm) of product edge contacts conveyor rails\n\nConveyor Design Considerations:\n- Edge conveyor straightness is critical (warpage must be <4mm to prevent \n  product falling)\n- Rails grow and warp during warm-up\n- Edge conveyors used for in-line processing or double-sided assembly\n\nUNIFORMITY REQUIREMENTS:\n- Bare board profile testing across matrix of points\n- Test points should include board edges (front, back, left, right) and center\n- Repeatability testing under loaded conditions\n\n================================================================================\n7. GUIDANCE ON SELECTING BEST RUNS BASED ON QUALITY AND EFFICIENCY\n================================================================================\n\nHIGH QUALITY SOLDER JOINT CRITERIA:\n1. Fine grain structure\n2. Excellent wetting characteristics\n3. Clean and solderable surfaces\n4. Small grain size\n\nFIVE PROCESS VARIABLES THAT MUST BE CONTROLLED:\n1. Materials - Fresh, compatible solder paste/flux system\n2. Machines - Operating within specifications, reliable, serviceable\n3. Manpower - Properly trained operators and maintenance personnel\n4. Methods - Defined procedures strictly adhered to\n5. Environment - Controlled humidity and temperature\n\nREFLOW PROFILE REQUIREMENT:\n\"High quality, low defect soldering requires identifying the optimum \ntemperature profile for reflowing the solder paste, and repeating over \nand over.\"\n\nKey principle: \"If you could hit the correct reflow profile using buckets \nof hot water, you would accomplish high quality, low defect solder joints.\"\n(The method of heating matters less than maintaining the correct profile)\n\nPROCESS BAND COMPLIANCE:\n- All solder joints on every board need to be heated similarly\n- Total temperature deviation from:\n  * Component thermal mismatch\n  * Oven loading\n  * Oven uniformity\n  Must fall within the defined reflow profile band\n\nDEFECT PREVENTION - STAY WITHIN THE BAND:\nIf temperature profile is NOT maintained within the band:\n\nPreheat Zone Issues:\n- Excessive ramp rate → Cracked chip capacitors\n- Insufficient preheat → Solder spatter and balls in reflow zone\n\nDryout Zone Issues:\n- Excessive drying → Paste skins over, vapor explosion causes spatter/balls\n- Insufficient drying → Solder spatter and balls\n\nReflow Zone Issues:\n- Insufficient heat → Non-reflowed joints, dull rough surface (cold joints)\n- Excessive heat → Burnt boards, damaged components, solder dewetting\n\nBEST RUN SELECTION CRITERIA:\n\nQuality Metrics:\n1. Profile stays within defined temperature band (e.g., ±25°C)\n2. Wetting time: 30-60 seconds\n3. Peak temperature: 183°C + 20°C = ~203°C minimum\n4. Ramp rate: <2°C/second in preheat\n5. All zones (preheat 100-150°C, dryout, reflow >203°C) properly executed\n\nEfficiency Metrics:\n1. Process speed must exceed minimum line speed\n2. Loading factor optimized (0.5-0.9 range)\n3. No bottlenecks at reflow oven\n4. Proper oven sizing for production rate\n5. Repeatability under production load\n\nEquipment Selection Criteria for Efficiency:\n- Proper oven size for production rate\n- Adequate process speed capability\n- Good repeatability (°C specification)\n- Maximum loading factor capacity\n- Appropriate conveyor type (mesh vs. edge)\n- Reliability and serviceability\n- Cost evaluation (initial + operating costs)\n\nOptimization Strategy:\n- \"Try to avoid becoming too focussed on one issue, such as populated \n  uniformity, as it may not lead you to the optimum decision.\"\n- Consider all factors holistically\n- Test and validate profile on actual production boards\n- Monitor: edge-to-center uniformity, front-to-back uniformity, \n  loaded vs unloaded conditions\n\nTYPICAL PROFILE STRUCTURE:\n1. Preheat Zone: 100-150°C, <2°C/s ramp rate\n2. Dryout/Soak Zone: Dry paste, activate flux\n3. Reflow Zone: >203°C (183°C + 20°C), 30-60s wetting time\n4. PREHEAT PHASE DEFINITION AND TEMPERATURE RANGE\n    10→================================================================================\n    11→\n    12→DEFINITION:\n    13→The preheat section is the first heating zone in a typical reflow profile.\n    14→It is used to fully preheat the SMT (Surface Mount Technology) assembly \n    15→before entering the dryout and reflow zones.\n    16→\n    17→APPLICABLE TEMPERATURE RANGE:\n    18→- Target Range: 100°C to 150°C\n    19→- Purpose: Bring the entire assembly to a uniform temperature to prepare \n    20→  for the dryout and reflow phases\n    21→\n    22→KEY REQUIREMENTS:\n    23→- The goal is to fully preheat the SMT assembly to temperatures generally \n    24→  between 100°C to 150°C\n    25→- Primary concern: Minimizing thermal shock on components\n    26→- Critical for preventing damage to multilayer ceramic chip capacitors\n    27→- Prevents solder paste spattering\n    28→\n    29→================================================================================\n    30→2. MAXIMUM ALLOWABLE RAMP RATE DURING PREHEAT\n    31→================================================================================\n    32→\n    33→MAXIMUM RAMP RATE: Less than 2°C/second\n    34→\n    35→SPECIFICATION:\n    36→\"The most critical parameter in the preheat section is to minimize the \n    37→rate of rise to less than 2°C/second.\"\n    38→\n    39→REASONING:\n    40→- Main concern is minimizing thermal shock on components\n    41→- Multilayer ceramic chip capacitors can be vulnerable to cracking if \n    42→  heated too fast\n    43→- Solder paste can spatter if heated too fast\n    44→\n    45→ADDITIONAL NOTES:\n    46→- At typical operating setpoints of 5-15% lamp power, heating rates are \n    47→  maintained under 2°C/second\n    48→- This controlled rate is critical for component reliability\n    49→\n    50→================================================================================\n    51→3. EQUATION/METHOD FOR CALCULATING RAMP RATE\n    52→================================================================================\n    53→\n    54→The handbook provides the equation for temperature rise, which can be used\n    55→to calculate and control ramp rates:\n    56→\n    57→TEMPERATURE RISE EQUATION:\n    58→ΔT = Q × A × t / (M × Cp)\n    59→\n    60→WHERE:\n    61→- ΔT = Product Temperature Rise (°C)\n    62→- Q = Heat Absorbed (W/cm²)\n    63→- A = Exposed Area (cm²)\n    64→- t = Heating Dwell Time (seconds)\n    65→- M = Mass of Object (kg)\n    66→- Cp = Specific Heat (W-sec/kg-°C)\n    67→\n    68→THERMAL MASS CONCEPT:\n    69→- M × Cp is referred to as \"thermal mass\"\n    70→- Greater thermal mass requires more heat to achieve a given temperature rise\n    71→- This equation helps predict heating behavior based on component mass \n    72→  and heating parameters\n    73→\n    74→PROCESS SPEED EQUATION:\n    75→PROCESS SPEED = OVEN CHAMBER HEATED LENGTH / PROCESS DWELL TIME\n    76→\n    77→LINE SPEED (MINIMUM) = BOARDS PER MINUTE × LENGTH PER BOARD / LOAD FACTOR\n    78→\n    79→These equations help calculate conveyor speed to achieve desired dwell times\n    80→and ramp rates.\n    81→\n    82→================================================================================\n    83→4. TAL (TIME ABOVE LIQUIDUS) DEFINITION, THRESHOLDS, AND ALLOWABLE WINDOW\n    84→================================================================================\n    85→\n    86→DEFINITION:\n    87→The handbook refers to this as \"wetting time\" - the amount of time the \n    88→solder joint is above the melting point (liquidus temperature).\n    89→\n    90→For 63/37 Eutectic Solder:\n    91→- Liquidus/Melting Temperature: 183°C\n    92→- This temperature must be exceeded by approximately 20°C to ensure \n    93→  quality reflow\n    94→- Effective minimum temperature for quality reflow: ~203°C\n    95→\n    96→ALLOWABLE WINDOW (WETTING TIME):\n    97→- Normal Range: 30 to 60 seconds for most pastes\n    98→- This is the time spent above the liquidus temperature (183°C)\n    99→\n   100→THRESHOLDS AND WARNINGS:\n   101→- Minimum: Less than 30 seconds may result in insufficient wetting and \n   102→  poor solder joint quality\n   103→- Maximum: Excessive wetting time (>60 seconds) may cause intermetallics \n   104→  to occur in the joint, resulting in brittle solder joints\n   105→- Optimal: 30-60 second window provides best balance\n   106→\n   107→QUALITY INDICATORS:\n   108→- Proper TAL results in fine grain structure and excellent wetting \n   109→  characteristics\n   110→- Insufficient time: Dull, rough surface finish; non-reflowed or partially \n   111→  reflowed joints (cold solder joints)\n   112→- Excessive time: Intermetallic formation, brittle joints\n   113→\n   114→================================================================================\n   115→5. PEAK TEMPERATURE REQUIREMENTS\n   116→================================================================================\n   117→\n   118→FOR 63/37 EUTECTIC SOLDER:\n   119→- Base Melting Point: 183°C\n   120→- Required Exceedance: Approximately 20°C above melting point\n   121→- Minimum Peak Temperature: ~203°C (183°C + 20°C)\n   122→\n   123→PURPOSE:\n   124→- Ensures quality reflow of solder joints\n   125→- Provides adequate temperature margin for complete wetting\n   126→- Ensures all solder joints reach proper reflow temperature\n   127→\n   128→TEMPERATURE PROFILE BAND:\n   129→- Example band size mentioned: 25°C\n   130→- The profile is not a single line but a \"band\" or \"process window\"\n   131→- Band size varies depending on solder paste, component type, and circuit \n   132→  board material\n   133→\n   134→UPPER LIMITS (DEFECTS FROM EXCESSIVE HEATING):\n   135→- Burnt boards\n   136→- Damaged components\n   137→- Solder dewetting (solder pulls away from pad or component lead)\n   138→\n   139→CRITICAL ZONES:\n   140→- Product temperatures greater than 150°C require high-purity nitrogen \n   141→  atmosphere (if using nitrogen process)\n   142→- Pure nitrogen delivered to reflow and dryout zones for critical stages\n   143→\n   144→================================================================================\n   145→6. BOARD GEOMETRY, THERMAL DWELL REQUIREMENTS, AND CONVEYOR SPEED CALCULATIONS\n   146→================================================================================\n   147→\n   148→THERMAL DWELL TIME:\n   149→Definition: The time a product spends in a heating zone (measured in seconds)\n   150→\n   151→From the temperature rise equation:\n   152→t = Heating Dwell Time (seconds)\n   153→\n   154→This is a critical parameter in the equation:\n   155→ΔT = Q × A × t / (M × Cp)\n   156→\n   157→CONVEYOR SPEED CALCULATIONS:\n   158→\n   159→1. MINIMUM LINE SPEED:\n   160→   LINE SPEED (MINIMUM) = BOARDS PER MINUTE × LENGTH PER BOARD / LOAD FACTOR\n   161→   \n   162→   Purpose: Operating at a conveyor speed greater than the calculated \n   163→   speed ensures that no product jams will occur on the onload area\n   164→\n   165→2. PROCESS SPEED:\n   166→   PROCESS SPEED = OVEN CHAMBER HEATED LENGTH / PROCESS DWELL TIME\n   167→   \n   168→   The process speed must be greater than the minimum calculated line \n   169→   speed to ensure bottlenecks will not occur at the reflow oven\n   170→\n   171→LOADING FACTOR:\n   172→   LOADING FACTOR = L / (L + S)\n   173→   \n   174→   Where:\n   175→   - L = Product length or spacing between products\n   176→   - S = Space between products\n   177→   - Higher values (0.5 to 0.9 typical) mean more difficult repeatability\n   178→   - Maximum loading factors typically range from 0.5 to 0.9\n   179→\n   180→BOARD GEOMETRY CONSIDERATIONS:\n   181→\n   182→Mass Differential Effects:\n   183→- High mass components (e.g., PLCCs) have considerably more thermal mass \n   184→  per unit area than discrete components\n   185→- Areas with high thermal mass are more difficult to heat\n   186→- Non-uniformity measurement: Thermocouple a lead on the largest and \n   187→  smallest device and run a profile\n   188→\n   189→Exposed Area (A):\n   190→- Larger exposed area (cm²) in the equation allows for more heat absorption\n   191→- Component placement affects heat distribution\n   192→\n   193→Edge Effects:\n   194→- Edge conveyors act as heat sinks on the edge of the product\n   195→- Edge heating zones may be needed to compensate\n   196→- Mesh conveyors reduce heat sink effects unless product width is very wide\n   197→- Only 0.17 inches (4 mm) of product edge contacts conveyor rails\n   198→\n   199→Conveyor Design Considerations:\n   200→- Edge conveyor straightness is critical (warpage must be <4mm to prevent \n   201→  product falling)\n   202→- Rails grow and warp during warm-up\n   203→- Edge conveyors used for in-line processing or double-sided assembly\n   204→\n   205→UNIFORMITY REQUIREMENTS:\n   206→- Bare board profile testing across matrix of points\n   207→- Test points should include board edges (front, back, left, right) and center\n   208→- Repeatability testing under loaded conditions\n   209→\n   210→================================================================================\n   211→7. GUIDANCE ON SELECTING BEST RUNS BASED ON QUALITY AND EFFICIENCY\n   212→================================================================================\n   213→\n   214→HIGH QUALITY SOLDER JOINT CRITERIA:\n   215→1. Fine grain structure\n   216→2. Excellent wetting characteristics\n   217→3. Clean and solderable surfaces\n   218→4. PREHEAT PHASE DEFINITION AND TEMPERATURE RANGE\n================================================================================\n\nDEFINITION:\nThe preheat section is the first heating zone in a typical reflow profile.\nIt is used to fully preheat the SMT (Surface Mount Technology) assembly \nbefore entering the dryout and reflow zones.\n\nAPPLICABLE TEMPERATURE RANGE:\n- Target Range: 100°C to 150°C\n- Purpose: Bring the entire assembly to a uniform temperature to prepare \n  for the dryout and reflow phases\n\nKEY REQUIREMENTS:\n- The goal is to fully preheat the SMT assembly to temperatures generally \n  between 100°C to 150°C\n- Primary concern: Minimizing thermal shock on components\n- Critical for preventing damage to multilayer ceramic chip capacitors\n- Prevents solder paste spattering\n\n================================================================================\n2. MAXIMUM ALLOWABLE RAMP RATE DURING PREHEAT\n================================================================================\n\nMAXIMUM RAMP RATE: Less than 2°C/second\n\nSPECIFICATION:\n\"The most critical parameter in the preheat section is to minimize the \nrate of rise to less than 2°C/second.\"\n\nREASONING:\n- Main concern is minimizing thermal shock on components\n- Multilayer ceramic chip capacitors can be vulnerable to cracking if \n  heated too fast\n- Solder paste can spatter if heated too fast\n\nADDITIONAL NOTES:\n- At typical operating setpoints of 5-15% lamp power, heating rates are \n  maintained under 2°C/second\n- This controlled rate is critical for component reliability\n\n================================================================================\n3. EQUATION/METHOD FOR CALCULATING RAMP RATE\n================================================================================\n\nThe handbook provides the equation for temperature rise, which can be used\nto calculate and control ramp rates:\n\nTEMPERATURE RISE EQUATION:\nΔT = Q × A × t / (M × Cp)\n\nWHERE:\n- ΔT = Product Temperature Rise (°C)\n- Q = Heat Absorbed (W/cm²)\n- A = Exposed Area (cm²)\n- t = Heating Dwell Time (seconds)\n- M = Mass of Object (kg)\n- Cp = Specific Heat (W-sec/kg-°C)\n\nTHERMAL MASS CONCEPT:\n- M × Cp is referred to as \"thermal mass\"\n- Greater thermal mass requires more heat to achieve a given temperature rise\n- This equation helps predict heating behavior based on component mass \n  and heating parameters\n\nPROCESS SPEED EQUATION:\nPROCESS SPEED = OVEN CHAMBER HEATED LENGTH / PROCESS DWELL TIME\n\nLINE SPEED (MINIMUM) = BOARDS PER MINUTE × LENGTH PER BOARD / LOAD FACTOR\n\nThese equations help calculate conveyor speed to achieve desired dwell times\nand ramp rates.\n\n================================================================================\n4. TAL (TIME ABOVE LIQUIDUS) DEFINITION, THRESHOLDS, AND ALLOWABLE WINDOW\n================================================================================\n\nDEFINITION:\nThe handbook refers to this as \"wetting time\" - the amount of time the \nsolder joint is above the melting point (liquidus temperature).\n\nFor 63/37 Eutectic Solder:\n- Liquidus/Melting Temperature: 183°C\n- This temperature must be exceeded by approximately 20°C to ensure \n  quality reflow\n- Effective minimum temperature for quality reflow: ~203°C\n\nALLOWABLE WINDOW (WETTING TIME):\n- Normal Range: 30 to 60 seconds for most pastes\n- This is the time spent above the liquidus temperature (183°C)\n\nTHRESHOLDS AND WARNINGS:\n- Minimum: Less than 30 seconds may result in insufficient wetting and \n  poor solder joint quality\n- Maximum: Excessive wetting time (>60 seconds) may cause intermetallics \n  to occur in the joint, resulting in brittle solder joints\n- Optimal: 30-60 second window provides best balance\n\nQUALITY INDICATORS:\n- Proper TAL results in fine grain structure and excellent wetting \n  characteristics\n- Insufficient time: Dull, rough surface finish; non-reflowed or partially \n  reflowed joints (cold solder joints)\n- Excessive time: Intermetallic formation, brittle joints\n\n================================================================================\n5. PEAK TEMPERATURE REQUIREMENTS\n================================================================================\n\nFOR 63/37 EUTECTIC SOLDER:\n- Base Melting Point: 183°C\n- Required Exceedance: Approximately 20°C above melting point\n- Minimum Peak Temperature: ~203°C (183°C + 20°C)\n\nPURPOSE:\n- Ensures quality reflow of solder joints\n- Provides adequate temperature margin for complete wetting\n- Ensures all solder joints reach proper reflow temperature\n\nTEMPERATURE PROFILE BAND:\n- Example band size mentioned: 25°C\n- The profile is not a single line but a \"band\" or \"process window\"\n- Band size varies depending on solder paste, component type, and circuit \n  board material\n\nUPPER LIMITS (DEFECTS FROM EXCESSIVE HEATING):\n- Burnt boards\n- Damaged components\n- Solder dewetting (solder pulls away from pad or component lead)\n\nCRITICAL ZONES:\n- Product temperatures greater than 150°C require high-purity nitrogen \n  atmosphere (if using nitrogen process)\n- Pure nitrogen delivered to reflow and dryout zones for critical stages\n\n================================================================================\n6. BOARD GEOMETRY, THERMAL DWELL REQUIREMENTS, AND CONVEYOR SPEED CALCULATIONS\n================================================================================\n\nTHERMAL DWELL TIME:\nDefinition: The time a product spends in a heating zone (measured in seconds)\n\nFrom the temperature rise equation:\nt = Heating Dwell Time (seconds)\n\nThis is a critical parameter in the equation:\nΔT = Q × A × t / (M × Cp)\n\nCONVEYOR SPEED CALCULATIONS:\n\n1. MINIMUM LINE SPEED:\n   LINE SPEED (MINIMUM) = BOARDS PER MINUTE × LENGTH PER BOARD / LOAD FACTOR\n   \n   Purpose: Operating at a conveyor speed greater than the calculated \n   speed ensures that no product jams will occur on the onload area\n\n2. PROCESS SPEED:\n   PROCESS SPEED = OVEN CHAMBER HEATED LENGTH / PROCESS DWELL TIME\n   \n   The process speed must be greater than the minimum calculated line \n   speed to ensure bottlenecks will not occur at the reflow oven\n\nLOADING FACTOR:\n   LOADING FACTOR = L / (L + S)\n   \n   Where:\n   - L = Product length or spacing between products\n   - S = Space between products\n   - Higher values (0.5 to 0.9 typical) mean more difficult repeatability\n   - Maximum loading factors typically range from 0.5 to 0.9\n\nBOARD GEOMETRY CONSIDERATIONS:\n\nMass Differential Effects:\n- High mass components (e.g., PLCCs) have considerably more thermal mass \n  per unit area than discrete components\n- Areas with high thermal mass are more difficult to heat\n- Non-uniformity measurement: Thermocouple a lead on the largest and \n  smallest device and run a profile\n\nExposed Area (A):\n- Larger exposed area (cm²) in the equation allows for more heat absorption\n- Component placement affects heat distribution\n\nEdge Effects:\n- Edge conveyors act as heat sinks on the edge of the product\n- Edge heating zones may be needed to compensate\n- Mesh conveyors reduce heat sink effects unless product width is very wide\n- Only 0.17 inches (4 mm) of product edge contacts conveyor rails\n\nConveyor Design Considerations:\n- Edge conveyor straightness is critical (warpage must be <4mm to prevent \n  product falling)\n- Rails grow and warp during warm-up\n- Edge conveyors used for in-line processing or double-sided assembly\n\nUNIFORMITY REQUIREMENTS:\n- Bare board profile testing across matrix of points\n- Test points should include board edges (front, back, left, right) and center\n- Repeatability testing under loaded conditions\n\n================================================================================\n7. GUIDANCE ON SELECTING BEST RUNS BASED ON QUALITY AND EFFICIENCY\n================================================================================\n\nHIGH QUALITY SOLDER JOINT CRITERIA:\n1. Fine grain structure\n2. Excellent wetting characteristics\n3. Clean and solderable surfaces\n4. Small grain size\n\nFIVE PROCESS VARIABLES THAT MUST BE CONTROLLED:\n1. Materials - Fresh, compatible solder paste/flux system\n2. Machines - Operating within specifications, reliable, serviceable\n3. Manpower - Properly trained operators and maintenance personnel\n4. Methods - Defined procedures strictly adhered to\n5. Environment - Controlled humidity and temperature\n\nREFLOW PROFILE REQUIREMENT:\n\"High quality, low defect soldering requires identifying the optimum \ntemperature profile for reflowing the solder paste, and repeating over \nand over.\"\n\nKey principle: \"If you could hit the correct reflow profile using buckets \nof hot water, you would accomplish high quality, low defect solder joints.\"\n(The method of heating matters less than maintaining the correct profile)\n\nPROCESS BAND COMPLIANCE:\n- All solder joints on every board need to be heated similarly\n- Total temperature deviation from:\n  * Component thermal mismatch\n  * Oven loading\n  * Oven uniformity\n  Must fall within the defined reflow profile band\n\nDEFECT PREVENTION - STAY WITHIN THE BAND:\nIf temperature profile is NOT maintained within the band:\n\nPreheat Zone Issues:\n- Excessive ramp rate → Cracked chip capacitors\n- Insufficient preheat → Solder spatter and balls in reflow zone\n\nDryout Zone Issues:\n- Excessive drying → Paste skins over, vapor explosion causes spatter/balls\n- Insufficient drying → Solder spatter and balls\n\nReflow Zone Issues:\n- Insufficient heat → Non-reflowed joints, dull rough surface (cold joints)\n- Excessive heat → Burnt boards, damaged components, solder dewetting\n\nBEST RUN SELECTION CRITERIA:\n\nQuality Metrics:\n1. Profile stays within defined temperature band (e.g., ±25°C)\n2. Wetting time: 30-60 seconds\n3. Peak temperature: 183°C + 20°C = ~203°C minimum\n4. Ramp rate: <2°C/second in preheat\n5. All zones (preheat 100-150°C, dryout, reflow >203°C) properly executed\n\nEfficiency Metrics:\n1. Process speed must exceed minimum line speed\n2. Loading factor optimized (0.5-0.9 range)\n3. No bottlenecks at reflow oven\n4. Proper oven sizing for production rate\n5. Repeatability under production load\n\nEquipment Selection Criteria for Efficiency:\n- Proper oven size for production rate\n- Adequate process speed capability\n- Good repeatability (°C specification)\n- Maximum loading factor capacity\n- Appropriate conveyor type (mesh vs. edge)\n- Reliability and serviceability\n- Cost evaluation (initial + operating costs)\n\nOptimization Strategy:\n- \"Try to avoid becoming too focussed on one issue, such as populated \n  uniformity, as it may not lead you to the optimum decision.\"\n- Consider all factors holistically\n- Test and validate profile on actual production boards\n- Monitor: edge-to-center uniformity, front-to-back uniformity, \n  loaded vs unloaded conditions\n\nTYPICAL PROFILE STRUCTURE:\n1. Preheat Zone: 100-150°C, <2°C/s ramp rate\n2. Dryout/Soak Zone: Dry paste, activate flux\n3. Reflow Zone: >203°C (183°C + 20°C), 30-60s wetting time\n4. Cooling: Controlled cooling rate\n\nPROCESS GAS CONSIDERATIONS (for quality):\n- Nitrogen atmosphere may be required for:\n  * Low-residue solder paste reflow\n  * No-clean processing\n  * Enhanced solderability\n- Purity levels: 35 PPM over incoming purity in dryout/reflow zones\n- Product temperature >150°C zones require pure nitrogen\n- Recirculated gas can be used in preheat zone (purity less critical)\n\n================================================================================\nSUMMARY OF KEY NUMERICAL VALUES\n================================================================================\n\nTemperature Specifications:\n- Preheat Range: 100°C to 150°C\n- Liquidus (63/37 Eutectic): 183°C\n- Minimum Peak: ~203°C (183°C + 20°C)\n- Critical nitrogen zone: >150°C\n\nTime Specifications:\n- Wetting Time (TAL): 30 to 60 seconds\n- Typical ramp rate: <2°C/second\n\nProcess Band:\n- Example band size: ±25°C (varies by paste/components/board)\n\nPhysical Specifications:\n- Edge conveyor contact: 0.17 inches (4 mm)\n- Loading factor range: 0.5 to 0.9 (typical maximum)\n\nGas Purity (if using nitrogen):\n- Dryout/Reflow zones: 35 PPM over incoming purity\n- Flow rates: 1800 cubic feet per hour (51 cubic meters per hour)\n\n================================================================================\n","numLines":333,"startLine":1,"totalLines":333}}}

°C\nPREHEAT_TEMP_MAX = 150.0  # °C\nMAX_PREHEAT_RAMP_RATE = 2.0  # °C/s\nMIN_PEAK_TEMP_OFFSET = 20.0  # °C above liquidus\nTAL_MIN = 30.0  # seconds\nTAL_MAX = 60.0  # seconds\n\ndef load_data():\n    \"\"\"Load all necessary data files.\"\"\"\n    print(\"Loading data files...\")\n    thermocouples = pd.read_csv('/app/data/thermocouples.csv')\n    mes_log = pd.read_csv('/app/data/mes_log.csv')\n    test_defects = pd.read_csv('/app/data/test_defects.csv')\n\n    return thermocouples, mes_log, test_defects\n\n\ndef calculate_q01_preheat_ramp(thermocouples, mes_log):\n    \"\"\"\n    Question 1: Calculate maximum temperature ramp rate during preheat for each run.\n\n    Preheat zone: 100°C to 150°C\n    Maximum allowable: 2°C/s\n    \"\"\"\n    print(\"\\n\" + \"=\"*80)\n    print(\"QUESTION 1: Maximum Preheat Ramp Rate Analysis\")\n    print(\"=\"*80)\n\n    results = {\n        \"ramp_rate_limit_c_per_s\": MAX_PREHEAT_RAMP_RATE,\n        \"violating_runs\": [],\n        \"max_ramp_by_run\": {}\n    }\n\n    #
